― What is happening, what is the matter with you? -she turned her accusing finger upon the table-. Oh dear! -was all my father said.

My mother went out and walked up the street, trying to find her calm.

― Lorena. Come here -I stopped halfway down the passage-. Did you do this?

― We all did it together. Bazel, Linda, Hassan, Mime, Szasza, Giacomo and I.

― Whose idea was it?

― Everyone’s.

― Well now!… And was it as amusing as you had meant it to be?

― Mmm… Yes.

― Lorena, do not leave your room. I shall go and inform your friends’ parents; you must all be punished.

So I did. I lay down on the bed and there I stayed, while my friends’ parents passed through the kitchen to contemplate our good behaviour.

A couple of hours later my father came into the room; the light of the sitting room came in with him. My father sat down beside me and, stroking my head, he said.

― …Lorena… Do you know what you have done?

― Yes, father.

― It was not well done. You have broken your mother’s figures. You are young and I understand what an irresistible temptation it was for you. But you must be measured; when you do a thing that is wrong, or that hurts somebody, whoever it may be, you must weigh whether you would like that thing done to you. Imagine how your mother feels.

― Bad.

― No; sad, and somewhat angry as well. Looking at her collection of ballerinas brings her lovely memories. And though those memories will always remain there, something she loved so much is gone.

― Do you think she will forgive me?

― Of course she will. She is in the kitchen; go and say something to her.

The girl went down the passage and stood beside her mother. The woman’s eyes were swollen with weeping. She was playing with the pieces, trying to fit them together.

― I am sorry, mother -she only nodded-. I shall not do it again.

― You have not left a single one whole. I hope you will have no such notion again.

― Do not be angry with me, mother; I did not mean to make you sad.

― It is done now. Learn from your mistakes so as not to fall into them a second time, Lorena.

― I love you, mother.

― I know it, my girl -the child went to her mother and they embraced; the woman kissed her on the forehead-. Away to bed; I shall see to clearing this up.

― Goodnight, mother.

― Goodnight, Lorena.

I had taken three steps down the passage when there was a knock at the door. I hid myself in the doorway of my room. My mother opened; it was Nereo. My father came out to receive him.

― Good evening -said she.

― Hello.

― What brings you here, my friend?

― I know what has happened. It was impossible not to hear your shouts, Verónica. I hope I am not disturbing you at this hour, but I wished the commotion to settle before paying you a visit.

― Our house is always open. You are welcome, Nereo. Thank you for coming -said my father.

― It was an excessive reaction. I left them at their supper and on my return I found my collection in this state. I am calmer now; they are only figures.

― Be easy, my friend. This is readily put right…

― There is no need, Nereo… -said my father-. Your powers should serve for weightier business.

― Do not trouble yourself, Marco Antonio; it costs me nothing. In exchange I want you to reason with Lorena rather than lay a punishment upon her.

― She is not punished. I have spoken with her already; we learn from our mistakes and go forward to be more responsible. She is an intelligent girl.

― Then there is no difficulty; that will be enough -he said, winking at the girl, who was half showing herself behind the door.

― The most important thing is to grow as a person: to understand, to learn, and to act accordingly. Such values will make our children people of great heart. Speak to the other parents, speak to the children. Do the exercise together, and let this mishap go no further. Agreed?

― So I shall -Marco Antonio went out, meaning to visit the other houses.

Meanwhile the alchemist was taking a few contrivances out of his bag. He drew an inscribed star, completed it with an endless number of signs, lit candles at each vertex and set the pieces in the centre.

After a while my father appeared with the other six children. I went out to meet them.

― Place yourselves about the circle, young people. I ask you to meditate upon what has happened today. Let us grow, so as to avoid committing errors that harm other people and, in consequence, ourselves. For my magic to work, you must have understood -the seven of us nodded-. Now let the eight of us take hands; I shall need your help to undo this wrong. Close your eyes and repeat after me: “what time has undone, sunk in chaos, let the magic of man remake, risen again in order”.

So we did, and on opening our eyes the figures stood there in one piece. The tapers had burned away, and the liquids that filled the bowls had vanished. A strange sensation, like a tingling, had run through our bodies; we were weary.

― I am afraid that to rebuild these figures I have had to take a little of the energy of each one of you, and by the taste of it, it is chocolate -the seven of us burst out laughing-. Mind your teeth and eat less sugar. Get your strength back with a little fruit.

» You may go home, and remember what we have learned today.

My father walked each of my friends back to his house. My mother was smiling as she set the figures on the shelves. Her smile was the best thanks the alchemist could have had.

― I shall go home now. I am glad that all is back in its place, and a step taken forward. Goodnight.

― Thank you, Nereo. Goodnight.

My mother came over to me.

― Lorena. Though everything may seem as it was, it has cost an unnecessary effort for which we must thank Nereo. His office as alchemist deserves greater matters. From today you must learn the lesson, and though you will receive no punishment, you will have to win back my trust in you over time. Go and sleep.

So my mother concluded that night. I went to my bed and she waited in the kitchen for my father to return.

Of that day I have little more to tell; I do not remember exactly how long it was before we got up to further mischief. What I shall never be able to forget was that first time I saw the alchemist work his magic.

Ω

Afterwards I was able to be present at some of his many mendings for the neighbours, but it would not be until some years had passed that his alchemy would be engraved upon my memory again.

I must have been some twelve or thirteen springs old. Everyone was present at that event which shook the village. Accustomed though we were to Nereo healing our illnesses and our wounds, this left us without words.

Shouts and voices between the houses. The neighbours were running towards the cornfield. Kírias was the name most often heard; something terrible must have befallen him. When we all came up, we could do nothing but confirm the omen.

Nobody knew how, and though he had other men with him at the reaping, it was not until they heard the cries that they found the lad run through by the scythe, his belly pierced from side to side. His mother’s tearing cries begged for help through her tears while she tried to stop the wound with cloths; the blood welled up beyond all holding.

Silence. Shock. Everyone drew back; Nereo, in haste, was drawing a twenty-pointed star about the boy. Signs upon every arm.

― Save my son… Save him! -the mother stammered.

Viveka was swiftly setting out the candles and the implements. With no more than a look between them they knew what the next step was.

― Lay the lad on the ground and step out of the circle… -the parents laid him down-. I know it hurts, Kírias. I shall do everything in my power to bring you out of this. First we shall draw out the scythe and then we shall close the wound, but I am going to need your help. I must be wholly concentrated, so that all may go well, Viveka will bind your hands behind your head and your feet good and tight. Agreed? -the lad nodded.

The young man’s gaze was vacant, without strength even to cry out. Nereo, placed at the master point, opened his hands over the earth, and the lines lit up. All around, the neighbours watched, absorbed. The alchemist was speaking in an indescribable tongue; and though I did not understand his words, the feeling of it said everything. That strange dialect invited us to take part, to concentrate, to send our energy towards the circle. The drawing shone brighter still and the fire of the candles grew. The alchemist’s eyes were wholly white and from his hands came a blinding candour. The lad rose slowly into the air, while the blade of the tool slid free of his body under its own weight. Kírias writhed, bled white, with cries that barely came to a whisper. A few moments of intense pain that seemed to run through us all. The scythe fell to the ground and the strain in Nereo’s face turned leaden, the muscles stirring under the skin; the energy the circle demanded was leaving us exhausted. The wound stopped bleeding; the young man did not move. The light, more intense with every moment, passed through the cut, narrowing it, healing as it went. The wound closed. The lad came down and lay stretched upon the ground.

Viveka took her place behind Nereo and waited. The light faded and the alchemist fell senseless, held up by his wife. She laid him down, set a cushion beneath his head, put soaked gauzes over his body and plunged the man’s hands into bowls of iced water; steam rose from his incandescent hands. Nereo’s breathing was disturbed. The parents came near; the boy was weak and at the point of death.

― Bring a litter and carry him home with care. The wound has healed but he has lost a great deal of blood; give him plenty of water and gruel so that he may recover. Make haste.

Several men lifted the lad onto a blanket and carried him to his home, followed by his parents and a few of the neighbours. As many of us again stayed with the alchemist, hypnotised by what had happened. I remember that a long while passed before Nereo could sit up; his face was weary, and he looked at us all.

― Thank you for your help, my friends. Alone I should not have managed it, but we must also give thanks to the rest of the living beings around us -nobody had noticed, but the trees, the flowers, the livestock, everything looked withered, without vigour-. Please, water and feed the earth and the animals.

His last word woke us from our rapture, and all the children did as he had asked.

I watched him from a distance. Viveka helped him to his feet, offering him a staff; he walked holding to his wife’s arm. The neighbours showed their gratitude by bowing as he passed. He smiled in thanks. He disappeared among the streets on his way home. There was a strange feeling in the valley; the sun lit the afternoon with a magic halo that could be breathed. I imagine the alchemist’s power went far beyond what he showed us from day to day.

Thanks to him, Kírias survived that mortal accident. A few days later we saw him ploughing the field again, with a smile and rosy cheeks. Images I shall never forget, and which nevertheless are small beside the great history lived by that unknown village where the alchemist’s sons lived.

Ω

Nereo being a man as selfless and as given to his fellows as I have ever seen a human being, capricious destiny chose to hold in store a future that, if not tragic, was very sad for the alchemist and unlooked-for by everyone. After the misfortune, the village that saw me grow and that branded unique events upon my memory was lost to oblivion, abandoned, forcing all its inhabitants to return to their origins and to keep the incredible secret of a thing that no longer existed.

I must have been going on seventeen; I was a woman grown, brought up with fondness and with love. A love that passed over the frontiers of blood among the people of the village. We were one great family, and in it there were two favourite sons, two little ones of five years, Arguestes and Zéfiro, twins: the one snow-white, golden-haired and blue-eyed; the other dark, with jet-black hair and olive-green eyes. Born of the alchemist and Viveka, heirs to their father’s legacy and future stewards of the village. Those children were loved by every neighbour: charming, affectionate and well brought up. And I shall not deny that their eyes had a hypnotic quality that enraptured. They came and went from the houses at their own will; every day they ate, played and slept in a different bed. Viveka and Nereo lived happy, watching their sons grow without want of anything. And if anything ill should befall them, they knew the little ones would be well cared for under the guardianship of every inhabitant.

The truth is that nothing ever happened to leave a bitter taste among us all. Nereo, one way or another, knew how to draw a smile from us, how to give us back our happiness in moments of sadness. It might be said that he was the spiritual guide, the elevated soul that showed us the way. And yet boding destiny chose to hold in store for that man and his family a dismal present. I do not know whether there is a god, but I am certain they did not deserve so sad a punishment.

It was not a thing known because it had been said; it was like a feeling, a strange foreboding, as though the spirit of that family could alter the very air of the village. Soon we all knew that Zéfiro and Arguestes had developed a tumour of the heart that would end their lives within a short space of time. Impossible; and yet the one organ of the body that does not develop such illnesses did so in these children.

Malena, a surgeon of some renown in her days in the great city, recommended taking the children to a hospital: unusual though the case was, there would be a possibility of finding donors, and should it take too long, of assisting the little ones by mechanical means. Several lads, Kírias among them, offered themselves as volunteers to give their own lives to save the children’s. The family thanked them for the gesture, but they could not accept. Neither offerings nor technology would save the lives of those little ones in whose veins magic ran. Despite the ill news, the children kept their smiles, almost as though it were no concern of theirs. They walked the streets as always, accompanied from one place to another by their mother, whose face made her worry plain.

For some days nothing was heard of Nereo. It was said he had shut himself away to study the means of saving his offspring. But a man like him knew well enough the means. Time vanished in that attic where the forces of the universe gathered to show the way the alchemist so longed for; Gaia pointed towards two twin hearts whose star was the same as his sons’. Nereo left his house in the night, while they slept, and said nothing. He walked three days crossing the great forest.

The following morning Viveka came into the room, saw the implements, and knew the reason for his absence.

Ω

Nereo’s steps went little by little into the unknown thickness, there where the hand of man did not yet reach. A narrow path seemed to draw itself between the trees and the hills. The way came to its end before a great cave.

― My most cordial greeting, clan of the wolves.

Silence. The man waited. Light footfalls approached. Out of the darkness came an enormous white wolf, the height of a man; her gaze was hypnotic, one eye blue and the other brown. Behind her, two black wolves, and between them, trotting curiously towards the intruder, two brown cubs. Out of nowhere, from among the undergrowth, the rest of the pack appeared and surrounded the man. Stillness. Nereo knelt.

― I hear you, Alchemist.

― I come to ask of you a favour beyond payment, great matriarch. My sons are dying and there is no cure to be had. Their hearts will cease at any moment. Both were born with the Gift; though sons of a human mother, they are heirs of my lineage. The only benefactors of the creatures that dwell within the bounds of our line.

― I understand, Alchemist. Your forebears saved us from the despotism of men, giving us back lands where we might dwell in peace. Our kind, and likewise the rest of the creatures that people the hidden forests, are in debt to your ancestors and to you.

» Tell us what this great favour is.

― The guides speak of two twins of your clan born on the same day, in the same instant, under the same star. Only the hearts of your children can save mine -the she-wolf looked at him severely. All waited.

― The life of my children is beyond payment, as you rightly say. But the lives of all the beings your offspring must watch over are worth, sight unseen, the sacrifice of my whole clan, if such were needed.

» The blood of my children shall give life to yours. So shall it be.

― Thank you, Tananka -said Nereo, bowing.

― I ask only that the essence of my cubs remain in your sons. They are no common wolves.

― I know it, and for that my gratitude is greater still. The spirits shall be joined, making one single essence, the harmonic balance between your children and mine.

― My will being yours, go with my twins and save the lineage of the alchemists.

» Etra, Aeco, travel with this man without fear. As you have heard, he needs you to save the lives of his sons. In time to come you shall become the guardians who protect the bounds that give life to your brothers and to your mother, as well as to the rest of the creatures that serve us for food, to the trees that give us shelter and to the rivers that quiet our thirst -the cubs nodded-. Go.

― Eternal thanks, Tananka. You may see your children whenever you wish; you need only call them from the boundary of the village. Now, with your leave, we shall be on our way.

― Farewell, alchemist.

― Farewell.

The pack watched the departure of the human followed by the two little ones. It was a goodbye that filled them with pride, but a sad one. The clan would soon forget the two cubs; new ones would come to take their place. Tananka, however, would never cease to think of those two children called to be part of history.

Ω

Nereo reached the village. Evening was falling. Everything remained as it was. In his house the lights were lit. Viveka was making supper; the children were playing at marbles on the rug. The father came into the house; the children fell silent at the sight of the wolves. His wife watched.

― Have no fear; they will do you no harm. They are your brothers; they have come to cure the sickness your hearts are suffering -silence.

― Nereo… -the woman drew a deep breath-. I find unfitting what I sense you are thinking to do.

― I know it, Viveka.

― We should speak of this alone, you and I. It is dangerous, and they are my sons too.

― It is already decided.

The man sat down in the rocking chair, lit a pipe from which smoke came in different shapes and colours, and began to relate what must happen within those four walls. She stayed standing at the other side of the room.

― Listen with care, my sons. From time out of memory the line of the alchemists has helped all those who have need of our Gift. A power born of the universal energies, of inert matter, of living beings; a granting of life in exchange for an exercise of goodness.

» After every ritual the alchemist gives over part of his own life, shortening a longevity that might otherwise reach several centuries. The blood that runs in our veins holds magic, a blood that, mixed with that of humans, becomes a remote possibility of being inherited. There might have been hundreds of children begotten and not one gift passed on. There might even have been one chosen, and yet the two of you possess a gift greater than my own.

» As a man, I cannot let my sons die; and as an alchemist, I cannot let two such powerful spirits of an all but extinct clan depart at a moment like this, when the human race is losing itself and mother earth is drowning past remedy. For this reason four friends created this village more than six decades ago; today it houses more than two hundred families of pure ideals and protects vast territories where forgotten species still exist; a village of which I am mentor, and of which you shall be mentors in time to come.

» The only way to save you is to transfer to you the hearts of these twin cubs, born in the same instant as yourselves. My task, however, is to give life, not to take it. And attending to the wish of Tananka, their mother, and to my duty as alchemist, I shall transmute you into a single creature, half man, half wolf, in which both essences will be amalgamated, giving life to a single being.

» Tonight you shall sleep being four, but tomorrow, at dawn, all will be ready for the transmutation. Do you understand what I say to you? -the four of them nodded-. You may go out to play. I must speak with your mother.

The children and the wolves went out to the yard. They looked at one another in silence. The situation was a strange one for all four. Though they accepted their destiny uprightly, they harboured doubt in their hearts. They knew they were not going to die, and they trusted in the alchemist’s power, but even the most elevated spirit would have faltered before such a trial. The children embraced, just as the younger of the wolves sought in the warmth of his brother the comfort he needed until the morning should come.

Nereo was making the preparations while Viveka set out all that was needful. A long while had passed before she made ready to speak.

― I am confused, and you have made me feel set aside. I want, as you do, for my sons to live, but I do not want you to take the lives of the wolves. More than that: I do not want our sons turned into creatures belonging neither to the world of men nor to that of beasts. Do you understand, Nereo?

― I do, Viveka. As to you, so to me the death of our sons seems unacceptable; but I am the last alchemist and I have two virtuous sons. My line is ineluctably dying out, and until the end of my days my duty is to watch over this small treasure we have made, to protect the life of everything that dwells within these bounds. To that I have given my life with abnegation, and the only thing I have, the only thing that is truly mine, is the love I bear my family: my sons and you.

» If they die, we shall die with them; nothing will gladden our hearts again and we shall never forgive ourselves. If they die, the race that walks between two worlds will die.

― Oh! Nereo. This is terrible; destiny punishes us and I cannot reach to understand why. Even striving to find your words reasonable, there remain many premises against them. You know that alchemy is an exercise of life; if you set yourself against the Reaper you will all be in grave danger. This is no accident in which you can tip the scales your way. Our sons’ illness has sentenced them to death; the universe awaits their souls. Do you mean to break the cosmic balance by stretching out their line of life?

― Their hearts are pure; never will the seed of darkness be begotten upon them. They shall not become monsters of death longing for a last breath in which to rest in peace. I shall see to that myself.

― You do not know what you say, my husband. They are children; however strong and pure they may be, however powerful your seal, you will be able to do nothing against that pragmatic grain of darkness that will lodge itself in their hearts, waiting for the moment to be born. Not only that: the five of you may die in the rite; and should you succeed, how many years of your life do you mean to give for cheating death? You mean to leave me a widow and in charge of two monsters for sons.

― Be quiet, do not blaspheme so! The decision is taken. You should be glad; we shall go on being a family for many years more. Your sons will do good and will care for all of us. Take pride in it. It is no selfish act, Viveka. How many lives have I saved, how many years of my existence have I sacrificed to put off the death of others. I am altruistic and I have given everything, absolutely everything, asking nothing in return. I find my happiness in being what I am and in my acts. I am like an apple tree that offers its fruit for the enjoyment of whoever eats it, with no ambition beyond that. But why may I not, just once, think of saving my sons from the unjust death that awaits them? I am not going to raise them from the dead, my wife; I am only going to give them a new heart -the man fell broken to the floor, weeping; the woman held him in her arms, in tears.

― I know it, Nereo, and I understand. I am no alchemist and I can do nothing for them, but you can save them. You are a good and generous man; your wish is a beautiful one and the universe will be kind, for all you want is that your sons should go on caring for mother earth and her creatures. I accept your wish and I share it. And though the darker course should come to pass, we shall be together to face our own destiny and that of our little ones.

― Thank you, my wife. I need you more than you imagine, to carry this through.

Both of them ceased speaking. They completed the preparations. All was ready. They came down from the garret and gathered the little ones in from the garden. They encouraged them with fond words as they took them to the bedroom. The children slept in the bed and the cubs at the feet of their corresponding twin.

After a while Nereo looked into the room; the wolves, curled up together, seemed to be awake. The man went quietly in and sat down beside them, smiling. He stroked the back of each of them.

― Fear nothing, my sons; I am here to watch over you, and so is your mother. Though from now on you will live here, you may always go back to the forest with the clan of the wolves. Nothing is going to change. Rest easy.

Nereo kissed each of the little ones and left the room. Viveka was waiting in bed, with one question before their rest.

― Nereo… I do not want you to leave me alone. How many years for this?

― It does not matter, Viveka. When I am gone, you will have your sons, and I shall wait until we are together again. I shall see it all from above.

― That is what worries me -she answered, resigned. She was not ready for what was to happen. Several hours passed before she could find sleep.

Ω

The sun was beginning to break, both bodies facing one another as on a sunlit night. It was the appointed moment. The alchemist placed himself within the circle, the four little ones seated at each of the cardinal points of the star. Viveka came out of the house and watched from a distance; the degree and colour of the light that came out through the oculus of the garret told the progress of the ritual.

From the alchemist there flowed a great white halo, peace; the children cleared their hearts of doubt and gave themselves up with full will, with love. The universe opened above them, showing them all truth. Space and time folded in upon themselves and the eight points of the star came together, North and South, East and West, turning beneath Nereo. Man and wolf transmuted into a single being, harbouring both spirits.

The ritual was coming to its end; the gates of heaven were closing, the circle had vanished, the candles were burned away. There were only two children beside the alchemist.

― Etra and Zéfiro, are you both harboured together in body and in essence? -the little one nodded-. Aeco, Arguestes, do you find yourselves both joined in body and in essence? -he bowed his head-. Now that your life begins, and promises to be long, your learning must begin. You will have to learn all that pertains to alchemy before I go. Do you understand, my sons?

― Yes -they answered together.

― It is time for you to take to this new life with love, with wisdom, with serenity and with diligence. In time your mind will be one alone, and you will accept your metamorphosis with joy, which will bring you to become unique creatures.

Viveka came into the house. Her two sons stood before her. She looked at the little ones closely; their features had changed. But Nereo had suffered the effects of the ritual. His hair wholly grey, his skin aged and wrinkled, his aspect weary. The woman drew a deep breath; an everlasting instant passed; she did not know her own family, and anguish ran through her whole being.

She remembered her promise: she would accept her destiny in whatever manner it came. Smiling, she embraced her sons and her husband. From that moment it was as though nothing had happened.

The following morning dawned. From the first rays of the sun until dusk they gave their time, with effort, to learning the art of alchemy. Brief were the moments when the twins went down to the village to play with the other children. Though the change was plain, the children did not seem to notice it; the adults, however, avoided any comment, at least until they saw Nereo some days later and understood the scale of the matter.

House by house the alchemist offered a brief account to quiet any dismay or speculation. It went thus: “my sons have suffered a terrible illness; the alchemy to treat them has been as demanding as it has been successful. Because of it our appearance has changed. From now on my sons will be the alchemists. I hope that despite our looks you will go on seeing us as you always have”. And the truth is that although the neighbourhood was a quiet one and little given to gossip, such an event could not escape comment. They lamented that Nereo had lost so many years and they questioned the strange aspect of the children. How terrible the illness must have been, and how great the alchemy to save the twins, if in saving the lives of their neighbours so many times it had never happened so.

Ω

With time everyone accepted it. Grown used by then to the new faces, life went on as normal. The twins learned quickly, more talented than their father; they exercised their powers whenever they were called upon. Nereo and Viveka watched their sons with pride as they grew like any other child. Those first fears of the woman had, years afterwards, dispelled, and contentment filled her heart.

Life in the village was pleasant for everyone. The years passed swiftly and almost a lustrum later, the twins being some nine years old and their appearance changed, what had to happen happened. Nereo fell gravely ill; within days his life was going out. He accepted the destiny he had chosen on the very day he saved his sons. At his bedside he explained to them the reasons for his going, asking them to do honour to their line, and that just as he had sacrificed himself for his fellows, so must they.

The children, sad and bewildered, took in their father’s words and accepted the destiny that had been entrusted to them.

― I love you, Nereo -said Viveka, holding him-. I shall miss you until we see each other again.

― Care for them as you always have. I shall watch you from the firmament. I leave you against my will, Viveka. But our sons remain, and that gladdens me at the hour of my going. I love you.

» The moment has come; I must go. Viveka, my sons, remember that I shall always love you and that my love will watch over you until the end of days.

That was his last word. Through that night they wept the want of Nereo. The next day he was buried in the garden of his house. Everyone came; in silence they remembered the man who gave his life in exchange for a better world.

Those were days of mourning and condolence. The neighbours cared for the little ones more tenderly than ever and wrapped Viveka about to keep her from solitude.

Time went on calming the wound, and from the alchemist’s mound splendid flowers were born, which in the looking reminded them that he was still among them. Every day Viveka cut one of them and set it in her hair.

The twins performed their offices as alchemists; they lived happy as one more among us, much loved by the people despite their aspect being by then no more than a sketch of a human.

When they turned fifteen, nobody seemed to have noticed the gradual metamorphosis. But as in every history, one day among so many there happens that thing, insignificant in itself, which must drastically change the events to come.

Ω

Dawn was breaking over the threshing floor where the men worked the wheat and the women gathered the fruit of the earth. By then I was already a young woman who, like her companions, was leaving childhood far behind, though in the village there were always new infants filling the streets with their glee.

The work was shared out evenly; there were rotations, divisions of tasks, and everything ran like a clock. That day two friends of my own year were responsible for minding the youngest. Rebeca and Lía were caring for five children barely a year old. Rebeca was warming the milk for the little ones while she rocked a girl in her arms, making faces that amused the baby. While she waited for the milk to come to temperature, her attention fell upon a bowl of sweets; she unwrapped one, broke off a small piece and gave it to the girl, who devoured it in an instant and wanted more.

The milk boiled. Rebeca sat the child in the high chair and left the sweet on the table. She put out the fire and filled the bottles, watching the little one out of the corner of her eye. But it was an instant only, and the girl, standing up on the chair, reached the sweet and put it in her mouth, to choke upon it. The retching alerted Rebeca. The milk fell to the floor. The girl threw herself upon the child, putting her fingers into her mouth.

― SPIT IT OUT! -she was shouting.

Lía, alerted, came into the kitchen; she did not know what to do. They struck the girl’s back; she was barely breathing, turning blue. The girl went out into the street calling for help; people ran towards the house. Rebeca turned the child face down and with strong pressings tried to force out the sweet.

It was a matter of minutes. Several women had come into the house. The outcry was deafening. They had managed to get the sweet out with a pair of tongs. They were giving her assisted breathing. Zéfiro arrived first. He tried to revive the girl without pause in his effort, under the watchful eyes of his neighbours who filled the kitchen; Arguestes arrived, and both gave the utmost. They needed only the briefest breath and the girl would breathe again, but that did not happen; the little one had died and the twins’ magic was already of no use.

The mother was crying out, destroyed. Rebeca and Lía bore, weeping, the string of insults the women pressed upon them. Feelings burst out in that air of helplessness. And a voice without an owner among the tumult said “this would not have happened had Nereo been here; he should be the alchemist, not you”. That rebuke, quickened by the situation, passed Zéfiro by; it bit into his brother, however, who went furiously out of the house. I went after him; his face showed his anger at those ungrateful words.

― Do not take it to heart. It was the tension of the moment. Nobody thinks so. Silvia has always been somewhat thoughtless, and besides she is the child’s aunt. Pay no attention, Arguestes; forget it.

― My brother and I give our lives for this place, for its inhabitants, for everything that lives here. My father gave his life for his sons and for you. It is not just that even in such a situation we should have to hear such a reproach. She was dead already when my brother arrived; we could do nothing.

― I know it. Be calm. The two of you make this village a wonderful place. Today is a sad day on which we have remembered that we are men like those of any other part of the world, and magic has not been able to work the miracle. Do not weep. Let me walk you home. Soon everything will be back to normal.

An enormous uproar inside the house. The mother and the aunt were shouting furiously at the girls. Zéfiro came between them and the tension in the family rose. They were abusing the young men with words. The neighbours held the women back and got the girls and the alchemist out of the house.

― Go home until we manage to calm this down -said one of the neighbours.

The village was piling up at the doors of the place. The five of us walked towards the house where Viveka was waiting, surprised by the commotion.

Rare was the occasion when such a thing happened, and Nereo’s voice had been enough to bring about calm. But this time the alchemists had become victims against their own will. The mother, indignant, went down to the house. She must have been there more than an hour. The people heard the woman’s words and grew quiet. The village needed to hear the voice of the matriarch, who still inspired the alchemist’s respect.

― You humble the figure of Nereo with your behaviour. He left the charge to Zéfiro and to Arguestes, both of them prepared and surpassing their father’s talent. It has been an unwanted event, but an unavoidable one.

» My sons are but fifteen years old; but never again fail in respect towards those who give their lives and watch over us from time out of memory until today, and in the time to come as well.

The hubbub quieted, and the next day the little one’s burial took place. The family apologised to the two girls for the harshness of the affair and accepted the girls’ repentance.

A short time later, with the excuse of studying in the city, Lía and Rebeca left the village. The feeling of guilt embittered them every time they crossed the path of the baby’s family.

That death was not in itself the significant thing. What that contempt brought about was the waking of the darkness Arguestes carried within, and which Nereo’s enormous sacrifice of love had held captive.

The fear of inevitable extinction the little ones harboured before the transmutation remained latent in their essence. And every soul stolen from death is called, sooner or later, by the shadows. Wrath and hatred unbridle the immanence, carrying the subject to a tragic death that returns his soul to the place where by right it belongs. Those ill-judged words cracked the dungeon that shut the doubt away. From that day the twin was not the same.

Both brothers were waiting in the sitting room for their mother to come. I was making them tea. Their faces were contrite, with the feeling of failure. I served them the drink and sat down beside them.

― Thank you, Lorena.

― There is nothing to thank, Zéfiro.

― Are we perhaps so ugly that we do not deserve the respect any other man deserves.

― No, Arguestes. You are wonderful. This has nothing to do with your looks. Today a baby has died and the family’s rage has sought a weak point through which to inflict hurt. You are young, and age believes itself entitled to upbraid you. They have done the same with Rebeca and with Lía.

» You are good men, possibly the best I shall ever know. Do not let this affect you; it does not deserve it.

― Thank you for your words of encouragement, Lorena. We shall remember this chapter, which will help us to grow and to better ourselves -said Zéfiro.

― We may need to better ourselves, but that family I do not mean to help again, not though they beg me. It will be your affair if anything happens to them.

― Be easy, brother; time will calm us all and normality will return. But I understand your resignation and I accept the proposal -Arguestes nodded, confirmed.

Viveka came into the house. She thanked me for the time I had spent with them, and bidding them goodnight I went back to my own home. The mother sat down between the two young men and, taking their hands, passed on the people’s apologies.

― You must forgive them. Understand that it has all been because of the grave situation. They have struck in the one place where they could: your youth.

― Youth is not a synonym for ineptitude, is it, mother?

― No, Arguestes; do not go on down that road. You are alchemists; your spirit is above the human. Cast that path aside.

― I know she loves us, mother. I know that many people in the village see us as their equals, and it gratifies me. But I know there are neighbours who behind their smiles hide fear and disdain towards our form. They think that because of us father is no longer here. We are monsters, mother. I do not understand why father gave his life to turn us into this; I do not understand why he consented to it.

― Be quiet, Arguestes! -cried Zéfiro, rising to his feet-. Do not stain our father’s name, nor insult our mother. Beyond what those people may think, in this house I have never felt different; I have been happy and loved, and so too among many of the neighbours, and you likewise. I do not understand such rage over so stupid a remark. If you do not like that family, I shall take charge of them. But you are an alchemist: rid yourself of those thoughts and be the pride of our father -Arguestes’ face was drawn tight.

― I am sorry, mother. I should not have said such a thing -he rose and went up to his room.

― I had never seen him so, mother; I do not understand. It is true the remark was wounding, but he is above such vileness -Viveka leaned back on the sofa, closed her eyes and drew a deep breath.

― There is something you must know, Zéfiro. I fear that today will be the beginning of an age of changes.

― Explain yourself, mother.

― You know the laws of alchemy better than I. You know that your father sacrificed himself for you. An act of love towards the dearest thing there is, a son. It is nothing you should feel guilty for; he is still alive and he is happy to see you grow. But in an alchemy such as yours death is cheated, and two beings are transmuted into one. A rite against nature that carries great risks. The universe claims your souls and does so through chaos; it seeks to stir hostile passions that may carry you to destruction.

» His greatest feat was to shut such feelings away in the deepest part of your hearts, so that they might never come forth; you would be cared for with special attention and fondness. By every means he had to prevent any ungrateful emotion from prowling about you. Sooner or later it had to happen. The rest you may imagine for yourself.

― Death will find the means to torment the soul that once should have been its own, until that soul desires to die and returns to its home…

― My son, your brother has been chosen first; I only hope that you will not follow his road. Now it is our task to care for him; we cannot allow his spirit to be corrupted.

― If father could shut the darkness away in us both, I shall be able to seal my twin’s heart again. And do not trouble yourself over me, mother; I walk the path of light.

» Now rest; go to your room and sleep. Today has been a hard day.

― Thank you, my son -Viveka kissed the boy and went to her room. Zéfiro stayed behind, tidying the sitting room.

― I know you are there. Have you heard it all?

― Yes, brother.

Zéfiro went out to the passage. Arguestes was sitting in the doorway of his room. Zéfiro looked at him, moved; his twin was weeping sadly, and he went up to embrace his brother.

― I am afraid… I do not want to end like this; it was not my fault. I do not know why I grew angry; truly I did not mean to.

― Be easy, Arguestes. We shall cut out all darkness by the root; have no fear. Feel father within you, and mother, and me. We are with you. Harbour no fear. Let us sleep now; tomorrow will be a new day.

Ω

Weeks later the village was again what it had always been. Every neighbour went on with his routine and the twins practised alchemy and were acclaimed for it. The truth is that after that unfortunate night there was no further altercation. Viveka and Zéfiro cared especially for Arguestes, who trained daily, seeking inner peace and driving off every shadow. And so the years went by. Those young men turned nineteen, and in their nature there came irrational desires that dulled their senses. Which was only natural, since in that period there happened to be more women between fifteen and twenty years of age than there had ever been in the village.

The twins were in love and the two girls answered their love. Every evening they walked together. The four of them posted on the hill, talking, laughing, playing. And so came the first kiss, laden with happiness. Time passed and both couples grew firm. So much so that little by little they began to let their affection be seen among the people of the village, and what was a rumour soon became a certainty. Viveka, glad for her sons, watched them enjoying their first love. It seemed that the tremendous joy of the alchemists echoed through the village, filling every daybreak with happiness. The years passed in a sigh and the four of them wished to celebrate an act of union, to make of the couple a family.

That same evening the two young men made their purpose known to their mother. The woman was beside herself with joy; delighted, she embraced them and celebrated their wish.

― Mother, there is something that troubles me.

― Tell me, Zéfiro.

― Will our children be like us?

― Ha, ha, ha -she laughed, amused-. Of course they will be like you: charming and wonderful. Do not fear for them; you are as you are because of the magic. Your children will be human.

― I am glad to know it. It is not that we think ourselves ugly, but we are aware of our condition. We cannot go out into the outside world, but our offspring must be able to -said Arguestes.

― Be easy, my sons; all will go well.

― We were thinking of going now.

― Go. All will go well -Viveka stayed in the doorway, smiling, watching them down the street.

Both walked full of gladness, agitated but confident. Barely a few yards before reaching the crossing where the two of them parted, Zéfiro stopped.

― What is the matter, brother?

― I have a strange foreboding.

― What do you mean?

― Perhaps it would be better if we went back home and put this off.

― I do not understand.

At that moment the girls’ fathers appeared, turning the corner. They came up to the twins.

― I am glad to find you, lads. Our daughters told us you would be coming by the house today, but perhaps it is better that we tell you this before bringing about an unpleasant situation.

― I believe I am somewhat lost; forgive our ignorance, but my brother and I would be grateful for an explanation that might clear things up -said Arguestes.

― You see, lads. Both families, after a long deliberation, have come to the conclusion that it would probably be preferable to wait a while longer before joining our daughters to you. You are very young, you are still maturing, volatile; what today is a yes, tomorrow is a no. It may be that in a few years they will want to go out to the great city; I do not know, an endless number of things. And a marriage may mean an unwanted tie in the time to come.

― I understand your words, but only this morning the four of us saw one another and we were agreed in coming to this point -said Zéfiro.

― Of course, my boy. There is no difficulty in there being a union later on; but for the present, go on being betrothed. We ourselves married at a greater age than yours. What we ask of you is nothing out of the way.

― Are they in agreement with this decision? -replied Arguestes, his face serious.

― Of course; after considering it with them, they have come to the same conclusion.

― I have the impression that all this is nothing but a contrivance to prevent your daughters from wedding us.

― No, Arguestes. Do not say that; there would be no greater honour than to see our daughters married to the alchemists. You know we respect and admire you. We are happy in a place like this thanks to you. You are a gift in these times. We understand your distress, but you are young and intelligent.

» You will understand that we came here escaping from the city; we mean to stay in the village until the last of our days, but our daughters have grown up here and the echoes of the world are too alluring to hold them. How long do you think it will be before they feel the desire to know new places? What will you do then? You do not know how it pains me to say this to you, but your place is here; you are the guardians of these bounds.

― And even were you to decide to leave all this and flee with them, you could not walk in the light of day; people would not accept you, you belong to a magical world -the men took the young men’s hands-. We do not expect you to share the decision, but we do expect you to accept it -the twins, disconcerted, nodded-. You may go on walking out together; enjoy it; but please, do not ask them to stay without first knowing the outside, give them the chance to choose.

» We are sorry it should be so. Now go home; tomorrow you will see more clearly and the four of you can speak about it. Thank you, lads, goodnight.

― Goodnight -was all the twins could stammer.

The men’s speech had disarmed them completely. Disagree though they did, it was more than reasonable. They stayed motionless a while, digesting what had happened.

― Hey, what are you two doing there?! You have turned to stone -I said, shaking them to bring them out of their daze.

― Listen, Lorena, will you ever leave the village?

― I have gone away several times already, and I do not know why, I always come back. I am twenty-eight now; after everything, I ought to find a partner and settle in some city, or so they say. As long as the work is temporary and I find nothing that obliges me to stay, I shall always come back here, to my parents, to you.

― So you do not want to stay here? -asked Arguestes, saddened.

― Of course I do, but perhaps later on; I was raised here and I must come back, but I should like to know the world, to find a certain stability. Even if it is in a small city, and when my temper is spent, to come back for good.

― Do you think everyone born here has the need to leave? -said Zéfiro.

― Each differs from the next. This place is wonderful, but I believe the outside world is worth the living. Everyone ought at least to know it, so as to decide where his place is. And by the way, why so many questions about the outside?

― We shall never be able to know it -said Arguestes.

― What do you mean, never!? We shall go by night; I shall take off that hair of yours, a good hat, a pair of large spectacles, and nobody will look at you.

― That may do well enough for sightseeing, but we shall never be able to make a family beyond these bounds. Lorena, we are monsters.

― No…! You are only different.

― Call it what you like -Arguestes returned, and she did not know what to answer.

Both turned and went back to their home. I watched them, motionless, wondering why life should be so cruel to those young men. But what I did have clear was that this silent feeling must prove more powerful than the shouting that once woke Arguestes’ wrath.

The twins came into the house. Viveka was making supper.

― I did not look for you until much later, my sons -on seeing their faces she stopped her words. Seated at the table-. Tell me what has happened.

― What has happened is that we are different… -said Zéfiro.

― And does that prevent you from falling in love, from having a family? Does it prevent you from growing and being happy?

― It prevents us from loving someone who is free to walk the world. A prerogative denied us in exchange for a prodigious sleight of hand. We may go on being a couple, but a union would bind them to this place, and though the decision saddens us, we find it just -said Arguestes, tense.

― Well, it is true you are young and that perhaps one day they will want to go; but until that moment comes, be happy with them. I know it is a harsh destiny, but you must accept it with pride.

― I do not understand the reason, mother: why I may not be loved and make a family as you did with father. Like the rest of the people who live in this place. What is special about it, why do we go on fighting to keep this sanctuary? We are the last of the alchemists; what sense is there in all this if the rest of the world turns the other way?

― Arguestes, do not grieve -she said, holding her son tightly-. You are the flower that is born in the middle of the desert. You must suffer the inclemencies of what comes, so as to make the world a more beautiful place. You are the germ of a better future. You are not different, but special.

» Do not cease to dream of love, of a family, of your children. Of your line walking the planet again; man will listen to his heart once more and respect Mother Earth. Do not be in haste; you have more than a human life in which to be happy. Give yourselves time. Never forget it.

― It is hard, mother -said Zéfiro-. We are children like the rest, and yet our responsibilities are tremendous: measureless sacrifices and meagre rewards. If the outside world is so interesting, do you believe we ought to remain here?

― I do, so I believe. And if those girls are destined to be your wives, though they go, they will come back sooner or later to share their lives with you. The love and the happiness one feels beside an alchemist one does not feel with any human. Whoever loves an alchemist does so for ever; believe me.

» Give no more importance to the union; it is only a formality. Love them and let everything come. I know the future holds an endless number of joys for you; trust in your mother’s words.

The three of them melted into a strong embrace.

― We need to go out and walk; we shall be in the meadow.

― I am off to bed; I shall see you tomorrow.

Zéfiro and Arguestes walked among the tall, cool grass, whose scent was intoxicating on that night of full moon. At the end of the valley, where the forest began, they rested sitting upon a boulder.

― Do you remember the day the baby died, almost five years ago.

― Yes.

― I managed to hold that feeling at bay with your help, and until today I had not felt its banished presence. But again I feel that confusion beating strongly within me. After this I find myself without strength; I do not even know whether I should go on fighting this anguish. It may be that I no longer wish to go on being an alchemist. Once it was no more than a fit of temper that I am proud to have overcome, but this situation goes beyond the moment; it stretches out in time. I should like to be one more among them and to be able to choose, or simply not to be -he said, full of sadness.

Zéfiro waited a long while before speaking.

― I share your affliction; I know how you feel. I, like you, am bound to my obligation above my happiness and my will. Neither can I leave this place. And today our mother’s words, comforting though they are, do not fill this emptiness. Whatever may happen, brother, I shall always be beside you; mother will be with us. And insufficient though it may seem now, it must be the ground of our joy.

― I know it, Zéfiro. But today the parts do not let the whole prevail.

― Arguestes, my brother, proceed by your own free will; but if we master the entity entirely and flow with the universe, every mischance will be overcome and we shall live happy. Walk another path and it will not be you who decides each step. This is very hard for us both; I am your twin, your reflection, I hear the slightest of your thoughts, I feel your beating a thousand miles away. Let us learn to live our destiny; let us not wait, let us accept.

» We are like a genie: infinite powers, a small space to live in, and chains that yoke our freedom. Perhaps one day somebody will free us with his third wish -Zéfiro smiled.

― Ha, ha, ha. I feel better after your words; however it may weigh upon me, I am bound to you and to mother, and my first obligation, before that of alchemist, is to return you all the love you give me. It is forbidden to surrender.

― So it is, brother.

Arguestes rose, and with his chest swelling he howled at the moon, letting himself be heard beyond his own frontiers. And though he had been convinced and his spirit was not sunk, the truth is that the feeling of helplessness, of bewilderment, was present, however briefly, on each of the days that passed.

Ω

They went on loving and seeing their girls; everything in the village went on the same. Great was the love their mother and all their neighbours bore them. And as was to be expected, the day came when Jané and Shaina wished to visit the city and discover the world. The twins accepted the decision. They trusted in their love after so many years, and however long the wait, they were certain their beloveds would return.

Both brothers swore to go on with their lives in a joyful manner. Longing for her. But if promises are always easy to keep in theory, the practice carries greater risk. And that unease at the departure gave way to anxiety, and time little by little gave way to the unfounded certainty that they would never return.

More and more often the brothers neglected their offices, wandering the meadows near the forest. Above all on the nights of full moon, when Arguestes spent long whiles howling, claiming the love that had fled.

Ω

The twins who in former days had been identical but for the colour of their eyes and their hair now, with the years gone by, grew ever further apart. Zéfiro’s face had saddened, his fair hair almost white. Arguestes had suffered a severe change; those small transformations, once containable and barely visible, now, listless and furious, he let come forth at their own will, deforming his features. Truculent, stooped, and with a broken voice.

Whether those circumstances were passing or indelible, the neighbours began to distrust the alchemists more and more. They attended to urgencies with ill will and reluctance, and soon distrust turned to fear. Only their mother was able to draw Zéfiro out to carry through his tasks; he limited himself to attending serious accidents, and received his neighbours once a week for trifles.

Viveka suffered that change which embittered her sons every day. She prayed for them and asked Nereo to bring them back to the path of uprightness, but nothing more could she do save love them. Powerless, she confined herself to accepting her sons’ will.

Arguestes wholly neglected his duty as alchemist. He disobeyed every order, ill-humoured on principle; he began to prowl the valleys and the forest half naked, like an animal. In his moments of lucidity he tilled the field, attended to the animals, sat beside his mother and read, played at cards with his brother; and when he did not, he ran deranged like a mad dog.

Ω

That night there was a full moon; the howls came from the knoll. Zéfiro walked towards it.

― Many years have passed, brother; too many since that day.

― True. Too many.

― When I look at the village, I see fewer people; they fear us; I hardly know the place our father left us. Perhaps this would always have been the village’s aspect had he been like us.

― I am weary of this, Arguestes; weary of this sadness, of this litany that has become everlasting. I do not feel able to bear the years left to me; I need a change in my life, I want to be happy. I have understood that everything we desire is within us; I no longer long for her return.

» And I want her to see us happy before her going.

― Who is going?

― Mother. You have not noticed it, but more than ten years have passed sunk in darkness, and if today we are still somewhat whole it is thanks to her. We have paid her no attention, but she has aged, before her time even, drowned in the sorrow of our misfortune; we have robbed her of the gladness she needs to live.

» How long is it now since you sat beside her, how long since you came into the house except to sleep a night now and then and barely give her a look while you devour what she prepares for you?

― You are no one to reproach me with anything, Zéfiro. Neither have you been an example to follow.

― I do not reproach you, brother; I only set it before you. This moment has had to come for us to value all the love she has borne us and how many tears of helplessness she has given us. Now I understand: our place is here, and that is not going to change. We can be happy, or live sunk in bitterness.

» Arguestes, mother is dying, I see it in her eyes -he sat up, frightened-, and she will not let us do anything for her; she would rather go than see us turned into this.

― That is impossible, Zéfiro! Mother cannot die -he exclaimed, bewildered-. What shall we do without mother? Nobody but she loves us from the heart; she is the only thing we have.

― And the sad thing is that we have squandered the time we were given beside her, wasted many years without giving back all that she gave us… -he said through tears-. I want to enjoy her for what time remains to her and give myself to the village as our father did.

Arguestes ran towards the house, leaving his brother behind. He flung open the door; Viveka started.

― Mother! -the boy looked at her; she was haggard for all her smile.

― My son! It is more than two months since you came to see me; your presence gladdens me.

― I feel wretched, mother; I am sorry that everything should be so. I am losing her, and nobody but you has ever made me feel so loved. I beg your pardon for having made you unhappy; you did not deserve such a thing. But I cannot fight against this; you knew it from the beginning. I could hold it back while I wished to, but now it is too late to go back.

» Zéfiro has opened his eyes and he will flood you with joy until your going, and all the love you have given us he will give back to the people and creatures of our bounds. But my ill has no remedy now; I can no longer, and I no longer wish to, and your absence will do nothing but torment this poisoned heart. Tonight I shall disappear into the mountains; remember me as I was some years ago, when I still sat beside you and fell asleep leaning on your lap. When you are in heaven do not look at me; I do not want you to see me thus. I ask only that you wait patiently until the end of my days, and when we meet again I shall be as I always ought to have been and you will be a mother answered; but until that moment comes I ask you to forgive me -the young man ran out. Viveka cried out her son’s name.

― Arguestes, do not go, stay with me…! -the woman stood weeping at the threshold; her son was disappearing into the forest. Zéfiro’s silhouette in the distance was coming home.

― I am sorry, mother, but I had to tell him.

― I know it, my son, I know it.

― I know where he hides; I can take you to him.

― No. Bitter as gall though it was, this has been his farewell. Let us go inside now.

Ω

I could not say how many months passed. Mother and son tried to be as happy as the absence allowed them. They made the most of every second until the moment came. The whole village attended the burial. Zéfiro stayed beside the mound day and night. My parents, myself and a few other neighbours stayed with him until well into the evening. The young man was left there alone, lost, saddened, and yet with a small smile that showed the joy of having had his mother until her last breath.

By some chance, I always had occasion to be in the village at the great events, though some of them were not to my liking. I remember that he was days sitting there; he did not eat nor drink, he spoke to nobody, and every night, from the depths of the forest, Arguestes’ howl broke the silence, lamenting his mother’s death. On the third night there were no howls; Arguestes came down from the mountains to give his mother a last farewell. Even in the darkness, Zéfiro started at the sight of his brother beside their parents’ graves.

― Tell me whether mother suffered in her death.

― It was a quiet farewell, without pain, happy. But her eyes let show the hope that you would come back to her side.

― It is not possible, brother, and the reason is plain.

― It surprises me to see you so. It is far from the image of the twins we once were. I am sorry to see you in this state. I can feel the darkness embittering your spirit.

― Do not fear for me; by this time little remains of what I was. I only wait for my hour to come, to be rid of this shell that corrupts me.

― Do you believe that when we go you will leave that blackness in this world…

― Do not play with me, brother. You know as well as I the counterpart to the alchemy our father worked to save our lives. For you it is easy to philosophise and to reproach my idiosyncrasy. You were not cursed by fear. I suffer the unimaginable; happy or sad, my heart is a torment. The Reaper claims my soul every day, and my longing, the one feeling that casts light upon this darkness, is to know that one day I shall meet my family again, and so in the beyond I shall be able to be happy, living the life that was denied me.

» This will be the last time we meet; I do not feel able to look at you and see myself reflected in your eyes. I shall live in the mountains. I do not wish to hear any rebuke or suggestion; as wise as you, so am I; different circumstances. I ask only that you keep watch on me when I am no more than a beast and have forgotten even my own name.

» Protect the humans if I try to do them harm. Resentment grows within me; believe me when I tell you that I shall seek vengeance -for an instant they looked fixedly at one another, finding the child each had once been-. Farewell, Zéfiro; be happy for me; we shall see each other in another life.

― Farewell, brother; remember that I shall always be at your side -he stroked his twin’s face.

― I know it… -he said through tears, and fled, losing himself in the forest.

Zéfiro went back home. The next day the alchemist walked the whole village giving the following message: Arguestes had left the village, and so from that day he would be the only alchemist, given to his tasks just as his father had been in former days.

So several springs passed. All returned to calm; the neighbourhood grew again and times of happiness were lived.

Ω

On one of those many nights, Arguestes’ howls were heard near the village. Zéfiro went up to the roof and looked into the darkness; everyone was asleep, but his ears heard in the distance though he could not see him. His twin was near.

A small crash; Zéfiro ran towards the shed beside the farm. A couple of men, half asleep, were coming up.

― Go back! -said the alchemist, running towards them.

The shed was wrecked, several animals dead and the tools past use. A pair of green eyes shone in the shadow.

― What are you doing here, brother?

― Grrrr… -a growl for his only answer. He took a couple of steps and let himself be seen. A grotesque creature.

― Arguestes… it is I, Zéfiro.

― Zefff… Zefff… Grrrr… -he threw himself upon his brother, knocking him to the ground, and fled swiftly to the woods.

― Go back home; tomorrow I shall put this right. Sleep easy; he will not come back tonight.

Zéfiro went back home; he looked through the window with his gaze lost in the forest, remembering the image of his twin.

― You do not even remember my name, turned into such a beast. I shall see to it that you do harm to no one, as I promised you.

At first light the people gathered about the yard, asking one another who had done that damage.

― My friends, what you see here is the uncontrolled rage of my brother. From now on, every time you hear his howls you must go inside -he said as he mended the disorder with his magic-. You need not fear further attacks on his part if you do as I tell you. I keep a bottle whose contents will repel Arguestes. With a single drop upon the key of your houses, an invisible barrier will be created that will prevent his entry even with the windows open, if the main door is locked with that key.

» Further, I shall shape a net of alarm about the village, able to detect my brother’s will to come near; its sound will be heard even before his howls. It will be time enough for you to take shelter and for me to hold back his advance.

» It is ten in the morning; I want everyone in the square at twelve to perform the alchemy. Tell those who are not here.

The stir went on among the neighbours, who spoke of what had happened with surprise. As the minutes passed they all crowded together to learn how to face the situation. The clock struck twelve. Zéfiro arrived, holding a glass flask in one hand. All attended.

― Alchemist -said a man-, before you go on, we ask you to make clear to us what has happened; we are fearful that it may occur again.

― I understand you, and if any of you wishes to go, he may. Nevertheless, while I am here, I promise that nothing will happen to anyone. I shall take charge of my brother myself. I shall move to the house at the pass of the valley; it is some five hundred yards from my own house, and from there I shall be able to keep better watch on Arguestes.

» My removal will mean changes. From now on I shall stay there, save in urgent situations that require my presence in the village. Slight illnesses, as well as mishaps of no importance, I shall treat at my new residence. Except on Fridays, the day on which I shall come down to the village for as many hours as are needed to put right the disorders that cannot be moved. If you have no question, please leave your keys in this bowl.

Almost all the neighbours did so, except for one or two who, after his words, chose to leave the village. He poured the liquid into the vessel and a circle of light formed around it. He conjured the words and the alchemy transmuted the keys.

― Let each of you take up his own. You know how it works. Now I shall go and make the net. I know it has never been necessary to lock a single door, but circumstances impose themselves. Remember: with only the main door locked he will not be able to pass, though the back doors or the windows stand wide open.

The people were bewildered; understand the new situation though they might, they could not set their worry aside. Zéfiro walked through the valley, carrying in his hand a large syringe from which he let fall a drop at every step, until he had gone entirely round the village. He set his hands on the ground and all the marks lit up; he spoke the words and the alarm was set.

The brew spent, he came down to the village, to the apothecary’s, where my father dried herbs, fruits and the like. The alchemist asked him to distil certain ingredients so as to stock himself with the liquid again, since its preparation took months. My father set to work.

Ω

The truth is that for some time the prevailing fear that every night shut doors and windows fast at each of his howls went little by little relaxing. Arguestes did not set foot in the village again, and nobody knew whether it was through forgetfulness or through Zéfiro’s intervention.

As had been agreed, illnesses and other matters were treated in the morning at the house in the valley. The neighbours carried victuals and supplied the alchemist. Only if he was required did he spend a couple of hours in the village.

And that very day, as he walked back home after his tasks, he met my father on the way.

― Good morning, Zéfiro. This morning there were only a few drops left to fill the amphora; it will surely be ready by now.

― If you would be so kind, I shall come with you and you may bottle it for me to take away -they went through to the dispensary. My father was dividing the liquid among six small flasks which he set in a basket. Suddenly there were shouts, a commotion.

― Have you called Zéfiro?!

― He is not at his house!

― And at his parents’ house, have you looked?! -voices were shouting.

― I shall go and see over there!

― Come, make haste, we must find him! -the alchemist came out into the street.

― He is here!!

― What is happening?

― Run, Zéfiro! -said a lad-. A horse has struck my cousin in the ribs and staved in his chest; he is vomiting blood and choking; he looks very bad.

― Come, run ahead of me; take me to him.

― He is in the stable; my uncle thinks it is the lungs… -the alchemist set off running, leaving the boy behind. The crowd drew aside at the sight of Zéfiro coming.

― Be easy, Santiago. I am here now; you will be well.

He laid him on the ground, drew a circle with a star inscribed. He set his hands on the boy’s chest; luminescence. Everyone watched, absorbed; it was years since they had seen the alchemist work on such a scale. The minutes passed, the wound healed, the child breathed quietly, the blood stopped flowing.

The silence was broken by a howl; everyone fixed their eyes upon Zéfiro. He waited an instant, the alchemy was not yet finished, another howl. Arguestes was crossing the line of safety when the small bell hanging from his wrist rang.

― Everyone inside, shut yourselves in! Come, come! He is still far off; make haste and sound the bell. Lock the stable door with the key.

The neighbours took shelter wherever was nearest, crowded together in the houses. After some minutes of terror, the silence turned sepulchral.

― Be calm; I must still finish the cure and he has crossed only the first line. We are safe here. I cannot be interrupted again.

He drew again about the lad and went on; more time was needed. The air was charged with nervousness, the seconds everlasting, and the bell rang twice more. The howl sounded close.

A black shadow; all eyes upon the figure; the muzzle showed through a crack, the enormous green eyes. Swollen with rage he threw himself at the gap, unable to pass through it. The neighbours started, in anguish, at every charge. Absorbed before Arguestes, whose face was past knowing after several years without sight of him.

Zéfiro went on with his task. After several attempts the beast gave up, losing itself among the streets of the village. The alchemist concluded his magic; the lad was getting his breath back.

― Thank you for saving our son, Zéfiro -he stood there, motionless, in silence; the bell rang again.

― Arguestes has gone; he has returned to the forest; we may come out. Now I shall go to the apothecary’s; I cannot allow this again, I shall set more barriers of containment -the people could not get over their astonishment. A woman came up.

― Zéfiro, that is not your twin, surely?

― I fear it is, Adriana, though he no longer looks it. Take care of Santiago.

The alchemist took up the basket with the flasks and walked briskly towards the house in the valley.

As he went he let fall a small continuous thread of liquid, going round the village three times, leaving ten paces between one circumference and the next. By the time evening came he was concluding his magic. He set three more small bells on his bracelet, keeping the alarm bell and one for each barrier.

The neighbourhood was overcome; the village had always been a quiet one, and now it was living moments of terror. Most of them, however, for the many years they had lived in peace, remained trusting in Zéfiro’s word.

The alchemist kept watch day and night, posted beside the window. He no longer came down to the village. Everything he did at his house, and always in constant alert. Though almost nobody noticed anything, Zéfiro’s weary face was the sign of Arguestes’ constant approaches. Despite the barriers that prevented his passage, his worry was plain, and more than once he had tried to make contact with him, without any success.

Ω

Calm returned to the village. Time had passed since Arguestes’ last visit and the neighbours lived untroubled, though outings had been reduced and were always made in a single vehicle armoured with magic. Since the day of Santiago’s accident nothing of note had happened. Except for the brief notice that came from the Alchemist’s house.

“Celebrating the summer solstice, the date on which my parents were married, the day on which they will have been forty-five years together. We shall give a small feast in the square, at which an alchemy of prosperity will be performed to draw plenty to the village. You may bring whatever you wish to have mended, transmuted or multiplied.”

It was a long time since anything had been celebrated, and longer still since the people had made merry gladly. Only two days remained and everything was prepared with care. A platform on which Zéfiro would work the alchemy upon whatever the neighbours brought, chairs for everyone, tables for the food and the drink, and banners from roof to roof.

The awaited day came. They ate and drank gladly; the music played. The twelve strokes rang and the crowd waited, seated. Zéfiro came up onto the boards and the spectacle began that so pleased them all; it was fascinating to see that consort of energies and lights fulfilling the wishes of the audience. Every object carried a small paper saying what was to be done with it, and so the alchemist, one after another, worked like the conductor of an orchestra.

Suddenly the objects floating in the air, transmuting, fell to the ground. Zéfiro stood motionless, looking into the distance. The first bell was ringing; everyone waited; the music ceased. The second bell. His face changed; he walked upon the platform, the neighbours on their feet, alerted. Zéfiro, with his hand open and held high, asked them for stillness. The third bell rang.

― RUN!!! Run home, take shelter anywhere and lock the door! Come! He has broken two barriers already and he will not be long in breaking this last one!

I ran towards the apothecary’s; I was going down the cellar steps when I heard Arguestes’ howls. I took a bottle and filled it with the little that had been distilled. As fast as I could I came out into the street; Zéfiro was walking towards the outskirts of the village; I ran after him.

― Wait, Zéfiro! Take this, make the barriers again -a howl close by froze my blood and made me stumble; the bottle fell to the ground.

― I have no power against him now, Lorena. Take shelter, and tomorrow let everyone leave the village. I cannot protect you beyond tonight. Go, away with you; he is here.

My brother Nicola ran to me and dragged me inside the house. Arguestes’ figure was drawn in the middle of the street. We shut the door and hid ourselves in the darkness of the passage. I could think only of Zéfiro going away, of the broken bottle.

― Damn it! We have not turned the key, Nicola.

Slowly I walked towards the sitting room and stood motionless; the wolf’s shadow was cast beside the window; I took a few steps on tiptoe towards the hall; he was going round the house in search of a way in, I sensed it with certainty; my steps slow, biting my lips, and the wolf walking towards the main door, his shadow cutting the light beneath it, sniffing.

A few inches remained before I could turn the key, and the knob began to turn. Terrified, I went back to my brother; without breathing, we went along pressed to the wall, trying to reach the cellar.

― And father and mother? -I whispered.

― In another house… -he murmured.

Arguestes prowled the sitting room in the half-dark, breathing deeply; a strong howl broke our courage. We stayed still; he knew we were there, he was playing; his slow steps going down the passage, smelling the fear, his shadow cast on the wall, his muzzle showing round the corner of the L-shaped passage. I shut my eyes as tight as I could and tried to shrink until I should disappear.

A thunderous wail broke the silence. Arguestes turned sharply and rushed out of the house; I ran after him and shut the door, turning the key. One of the baker’s daughters was crying in the middle of the street; her mother was running towards her. I do not know how the girl came to be there, but her weeping was a lure to the wolf, his face contorted, almost amused.

The mother was coming near her daughter and Arguestes was doing the same from the other flank. Several men came out into his path, armed with sickles and scythes. The beast did not fear them; the woman took the girl and sheltered in the house. The men backed away without lowering their guard. A shriek went with the sickle that flew, grazing the beast, enough to make it hide. Arguestes shook the door and tried to come in by every opening, but he could not.

Zéfiro used all his magic to strengthen the houses. Arguestes, after trying every dwelling one by one, after wrecking all he could and killing every animal, took to the forest, letting his howl be heard.

The next day everyone abandoned the village; no word was needed; what had happened was enough to convince us of our going. And however sad it was to leave our houses, though there was nowhere to go, fear pushed us without hesitation.

Ω

Zéfiro, I imagine, still lives there, near his brother. I only hope that they are well. Of many of my neighbours I know little. My family lives in the city; we seldom speak of all that, though the memory of those years is still present.

The End
